Certain References and Information
This report is prepared for overseas investors and compiled based on contents of the Annual Securities Report 
(“Yukashoken Hokokusho”) of Mitsui & Co., Ltd. filed with the Director-General of the Kanto Local Finance Bureau of the 
Ministry of Finance of Japan on June 12, 2026.
As used in this report, “Mitsui,” “Mitsui & Co.” and the “Company” are used to refer to Mitsui & Co., Ltd. (Mitsui Bussan 
Kabushiki Kaisha), and the “Group” and the “Mitsui & Co. Group” are used to indicate Mitsui & Co., Ltd. and its 
consolidated subsidiaries, unless otherwise indicated. “Share” means one share of Mitsui’s common stock, “ADS” means 
an American Depositary Share representing 20 shares, and “ADR” means an American Depositary Receipt evidencing 
one or more ADSs. Also, “dollar” or “$” means the lawful currency of the United States of America, and “yen,” “Yen” or “¥” 
means the lawful currency of Japan.
All financial statements and information contained in this report have been prepared in accordance with IFRS Accounting 
Standards as issued by the International Accounting Standards Board except where otherwise noted.
A Cautionary Note on Forward-Looking Statements
This report includes forward-looking statements based on our current expectations, assumptions, estimates and 
projections about our business, our industry and capital markets around the world. Generally, these forward-looking 
statements can be identified by the use of forward-looking terminology such as “may,” “expect,” “anticipate,” “estimate,” 
“forecast,” “plan” or similar words. The forward-looking statements in this report are subject to various risks, uncertainties 
and assumptions. These statements discuss future expectations, identify strategies, contain projections of results of 
operations or our financial position, or state other forward-looking information. Known and unknown risks, uncertainties 
and other factors could cause our actual operating results to differ materially from those contained or implied in any 
forward-looking statement. Our expectations expressed in these forward-looking statements may not turn out to be correct, 
and our actual results could materially differ from and be worse than our expectations.
Important risks and factors that could cause our actual results to differ materially from our expectations are discussed in “2. 
Operating and Financial Review and Prospects, 3. Risk Factors” or elsewhere in this report and include, without limitation:
•  changes in economic conditions that may lead to unforeseen developments in markets for products handled by us;
•  fluctuations in currency exchange rates that may cause unexpected deterioration in the value of transactions;
•  adverse political developments in the various jurisdictions where we operate, which among things, may create 
delays or postponements of transactions and projects;
•  changes in laws, regulations or policies in any of the countries where we conduct our operations; and
•  significant changes in the competitive environment.
We do not assume, and specifically disclaim, any obligation to update any forward-looking statements which speak only as 
of the date made.

per share”, “Basic earnings per share attributable to owners of the parent” and “Diluted earnings per share 
attributable to owners of the parent” are calculated as if the share split occurred at the beginning of the 
103rd fiscal year.
3.  The Total Shareholder Return are the ratio as calculated by dividing the sum of the stock price at the end of 
each fiscal year and the cumulative dividends per share from the fiscal year ended March 31, 2022 to each 
fiscal year, by the stock price at the end of the year ended Mar 31, 2021.
4.  The highest and lowest share prices are those quoted on the Prime Market of the Tokyo Stock Exchange 
from April 4, 2022, and prior to that date, the prices are for the first section of the Tokyo Stock Exchange. 
The highest and lowest share prices of the 106th are those after the share split and the highest and lowest 
share prices prior to the share split are indicated in parentheses.

Jul. 1947 Daiichi Bussan Kabushiki Kaisha established with common stock of ¥195,000
May 1949 Listed on the Tokyo Stock Exchange
Feb. 1959 Changed the name to Mitsui Bussan Kabushiki Kaisha (Mitsui & Co., Ltd.)
Jan. 1963 Participated in the development of the Moura Coal Mine in Australia (currently the Dawson Coal Mine)
May 1963 Issued American Depositary Receipts (ADR) (registered on NASDAQ in the U.S. in February 1971)
Feb. 1965 Participated in Robe River iron mine in Australia
Apr. 1966 Established Mitsui & Co. (U.S.A.)
Oct. 1966 Concluded long-term purchase agreement of iron ore from Mount Newman in Australia
Feb. 1971 Participated in MBR in Brazil (underwent restructuring through M&A, leading to the current Vale)
Mar. 1971 Split off lease business and established Mitsui Leasing, Ltd. (currently JA Mitsui Leasing)
Sept. 1971 Signed basic agreement on development of Liquefied Natural Gas (LNG) in Das Island, Abu Dhabi 
(currently ADNOC LNG)
Nov. 1976 Moved head office to Otemachi, Chiyoda-ku, Tokyo
Dec. 1990 Concluded Iran Petrochemical Project due to winding up of Iran Chemical Development (Signed basic 
agreement in 1971)
Oct. 1991 Introduced Chief Operating Officer system
Apr. 1993 Established Mitsui & Co. Steel Construction Material Ltd. (currently Mitsui & Co. Steel Ltd.)
Jun. 1994 Signed development contracts (production sharing contract) for the Sakhalin II petroleum and natural 
gas projects
Feb. 2001 Invested in Penske Automotive Group, an automobile sales and comprehensive solution service 
company
Apr. 2002 Introduced Managing Officer system
Jun. 2003 First appointment of (one) external director
Apr. 2006 Introduced overseas regional business unit system
Feb. 2010 Participated in the Marcellus Shale Gas production development project in the U.S.
Apr. 2011 Delisted from NASDAQ (deregistered from the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission in July 2011)
Feb. 2014 Participated in Fairway Methanol in the U.S.
Oct. 2014 Participated in Cameron LNG project in the U.S.
Mar. 2019 Became the largest shareholder of IHH Healthcare in Malaysia through an additional investment (Initially 
invested in 2011)
May 2020 Completion of the new head office building
Apr. 2022 Transitioned from 1st Section of the Tokyo Stock Exchange to the Prime Market
Apr. 2023 Aim Services, a major contract food service company in Japan became a wholly owned subsidiary of 
Mitsui (established in 1976)
Oct. 2025 Participated in Rhodes Ridge iron ore project in Australia
2. History
Mitsui Bussan Kabushiki Kaisha (“Mitsui & Co., Ltd.” in English) was originally incorporated on July 25, 1947, as Daiichi 
Bussan Kabushiki Kaisha with a common stock of ¥195,000, with the main purpose of importing, exporting and selling a 
wide variety of products.
Since our establishment, our business results have developed strongly, and we have grown in scale as a result of 
capital increases or stock dividends, the issuance of foreign currency-denominated and domestic convertible bonds, as 
well as integration with other new companies. On February 16, 1959, we changed our name to our present name of 
Mitsui Bussan Kabushiki Kaisha (Mitsui & Co., Ltd.), and took the form of a general trading company in both name and 
practice. From then until the present day, we have continued to expand our business through mergers and acquisitions 
of other businesses and companies.
The significant developments for the companies that occurred during this time, including name changes, mergers, 
establishment of major affiliated companies, listings on securities exchanges, and other, are as follows:

3. Business Overview
In each area including Mineral & Metal Resources, Energy, Machinery & Infrastructure, Chemicals, Iron & Steel 
Products, Lifestyle, and Innovation & Corporate Development, the Company and its consolidated subsidiaries engage in 
a diversified range of businesses, including the trading of various commodities, manufacturing, transportation, and 
financing, making full use of our global operations network and ability to leverage information, with Mitsui & Co. as the 
global investment and trading company at the center of it all. Furthermore, the Company and its consolidated 
subsidiaries engage in a wide range of initiatives that include the development of natural resources and infrastructure 
projects, business investments related to the environment, new technologies, next-generation fuels and wellness, and 
value creation that leverages digital tools.
The Company assigns headquarters business units by business in the head office. Each headquarters business unit 
drafts overall strategies that unify the regional business units and blocs internally and externally for each business area 
in respect of which they are in charge and develops business activities for the entire world with affiliated companies. As 
a driver of the regional strategy, the regional business units and blocs are responsible for the regions they are in charge 
of, and cooperate with the headquarters business units while carrying out broad and diversified business along with 
affiliated companies under each umbrella.
For the disclosure pursuant to IFRS Accounting Standards 8 “Operating Segments,” these headquarters business units 
and regional business units are organized into seven business segments based on the business domains taking into 
account managerial decisions relating to allocation of resources, assessment of such operating performance, and the 
products or services they handle.
We have 446 affiliated companies for consolidation, which consist of 206 overseas subsidiaries, 72 domestic 
subsidiaries, 136 overseas equity accounted investees and 32 domestic equity accounted investees.

2. Operating and Financial Review and Prospects
1. Management Policies, Operating Environment, and Management Issues
This section contains forward-looking statements about Mitsui and its consolidated subsidiaries. These forward-looking 
statements involve assumptions, risks, uncertainties and other factors, including, but not limited to, those described in “3. 
Risk Factors.” Such risks, uncertainties and other factors may cause our actual results to be materially different from any 
future results expressed or implied by these forward-looking statements.
(1) Review of Medium-term Management Plan 2026
Over the three years of Medium-term Management Plan (MTMP) 2026, Creating Sustainable Futures (announced in 
May 2023), amid significant changes in the external environment, we worked to strengthen existing businesses, improve 
efficiency and execute turnarounds, and develop new businesses. By steadily advancing each initiative, we enhanced 
base profit and were able to expand our global and high-quality business portfolio.
As a result, for the fiscal year ended March 2026, profit (attributable to owners of the parent; hereinafter used with the 
same meaning, including in figures) reached 834.0 billion yen, Core Operating Cash Flow (COCF) reached the 1 trillion 
yen level for the fifth consecutive fiscal year, and ROE averaged 12.5% over the three-year period. Shareholder returns 
as a percentage of COCF is forecasted* to exceed 53%.
* This is based on the assumption that the year-end dividend will be resolved at the Ordinary General Meeting of 
Shareholders on June 17, 2026
The three Key Strategic Initiatives defined in MTMP2026 also progressed steadily. Toward the next stage of growth, we 
executed approximately 2.4 trillion yen in investments for growth, including projects such as the acquisition of interest in 
the Rhodes Ridge iron ore project in Australia, one of the world’s largest undeveloped iron ore deposits. In addition, 
capital generated in excess of the initial plan through flexible asset recycling and other inflows were allocated in a well-
balanced manner between investments for growth and shareholder returns, including dividends and share repurchases. 
Through this approach, we improved our business portfolio and strengthened shareholder returns.

--- page 16 ---
Environmental
understanding
- Continuous increase in demand for materials and resources driven by population growth, global 
economic growth and the widespread adoption of AI.
- Energy transformation towards a decarbonized society, including EVs and electrification, and the 
need for critical minerals found only in certain locations across the world
- Increasing response to climate change, natural capital and human rights-related matters in 
mining operations and supply chains
Risks Opportunities
- Impact to demand for resources following Chinese 
economic slowdown
- Impact of higher operating costs due to inflation and 
high interest rates
- Impact of technological innovation and government 
policies on the supply, demand and commodity prices
- Demand increase for low-carbon materials (including 
ferrous materials), including recycling
- Continuous growth of India and Southeast Asia as 
metal resources demand areas and the potential of 
Africa as a resources supply area
(2) Operating Environment
1) General
Note: The following describes the recognition of the economic environments as of May 2026.
Descriptions included herein may differ from our current recognition.
In the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, the global economy saw a gradual pick up, supported by a surge in capital 
investment related to AI, despite downward pressure from US tariff policies. However, toward the end of the fiscal 
year, transit restrictions in the Strait of Hormuz stemming from a deterioration in the situation in the Middle East led to 
disruptions in the supply of energy and other resources, causing a rapid intensification of concerns about an economic 
slowdown.
In the US economy, although employment growth slowed, consumer spending remained relatively firm due to the 
wealth effect from rising stock prices, and capital investment—particularly  in AI-related areas—increased,  resulting in 
a continued recovery. The European economy showed signs of an overall recovery. While stagnation persisted in 
Germany, Southern European countries such as Spain performed well. In the Chinese economy, exports grew to 
markets other than the US, but with the real estate market remaining sluggish, domestic demand including 
consumption was weak, and overall performance remained subdued. The Japanese economy saw steady consumer 
spending and capital investment, and the economy was steadily recovering. However, toward the end of the fiscal 
year, consumer sentiment deteriorated rapidly in response to rising tensions in the Middle East, and signs of a change 
in momentum emerged.
The outlook for the global economy is expected to be heavily influenced by developments in the Middle East, and 
close attention to future trends related to geopolitical risks is required.

environment are as follows.

understanding

power sources by country and region, rising demand for electricity and digital infrastructure due 
to digitalization

a normalization phase
- Although the pace varies by country and region, the shift to transportation with lower 
environmental impact is expected to continue
- Geopolitical risks, including tensions in the Middle East, have led to a sharp rise in tanker market

next-generation vessels expected to progress in

(3) Medium-term Management Plan 2029
1) Our Vision
In addition to the materialization of geopolitical risks, ongoing changes driven by the environment and energy, 
lifestyles, and new technologies have resulted in an uncertain business environment. Under these conditions, we 
continue to evolve our global portfolio, enhance integrated risk management, and pursue ongoing transformation of 
our business model through innovation. By providing real solutions to social challenges and ensuring the stable supply 
of products and services tailored to customer needs, we will fulfill our role in society and contribute to building brighter 
futures around the world.
We recently formulated Medium-term Management Plan (MTMP) 2029, 
pathway to 2030 and beyond, shaping futures 
through trust and innovation. This plan sets out the pathway for the next three years toward realizing our medium- to 
long-term vision for 2030 and beyond. Based on a reproducible value creation framework, we will continue to enhance 
the quality of our portfolio while combining our professional talent with AI’s exploratory power to realize 
nonlinear 
combinatory value. Through this, we aim to establish a virtuous cycle between enhancing corporate value and tackling 
social challenges earning trust from stakeholders.

2. Operating and Financial Review and Prospects 1. Management Policies, Operating Environment, and Management Issues (2) Operating Environment   continued

--- page 20 ---
2) Quantitative Targets for Medium-term Management Plan 2029 and Vision for 2030
For the fiscal year ending March 2029, we have set record-high targets of COCF of 1.2 trillion yen, profit (attributable 
to owners of the parent; hereinafter used with the same meaning, including in figures) of 1.1 trillion yen, and ROE of 
12%. We are targeting shareholder returns as a percentage of COCF at the 50% level for the MTMP2029 period.
As our vision for 2030, we aim to achieve profit exceeding 1.4 trillion yen and ROE exceeding 13%.
3) Pathway to 2030 and Beyond
In MTMP2029, we have newly defined our Corporate Strategy around three pillars: distinctive competitive advantages, 
continuously transforming earnings base, and value creation driven by capable individuals. Based on the evolved Key 
Strategic Initiatives described below, we aim to realize our vision for 2030.
To support these efforts, we have identified 
sustainability management , wellbeing and health  & safety , and HR 
strategy as priority themes, and will continue to strengthen our management foundation over the long term.
- 17 -
2. Operating and Financial Review and Prospects 1. Management Policies, Operating Environment, and Management Issues (3) Medium-term Management Plan 2029   continued

--- page 21 ---
4) Evolved Key Strategic Initiatives
We will evolve the three Key Strategic Initiatives defined in MTMP 2026 to achieve further growth. Under Industrial 
Business Solutions 2.0, we will expand stable supply platforms in the iron ore and copper domains, capture additional 
high-quality opportunities, evolve our business foundation in mobility and create new businesses by capturing 
structural shifts in industry. Under Global Energy Transformation 2.0, we will advance initiatives across the entire 
value chain, from the supply of energy to the provision and utilization of compute, while optimizing energy supply to 
meet increasingly diverse needs. Under 
Wellness Ecosystem Creation 2.0, we will pursue new domains such as drug 
discovery support by leveraging data accumulated through our hospital business, while further strengthening our 
protein business group and expanding into adjacent areas.
        ・Actual results are shown in approximate amounts in units of 10 billion yen
5) Capital Allocation
In an uncertain business environment, we will secure a wide range of management options and allocate capital in a 
strategic and dynamic manner. In addition to a robust COCF base, we will further enhance asset value through 
evolved 
middle-game initiatives and expand the Management Allocation by executing flexible and timely asset 
recycling. While taking into account long-term capital efficiency and financial soundness, we will allocate capital to 
highly competitive investments for growth and shareholder returns.
* The Management Allocation refers to funds allocated strategically to investments for growth and shareholder returns.
- 18 -
2. Operating and Financial Review and Prospects 1. Management Policies, Operating Environment, and Management Issues (3) Medium-term Management Plan 2029   continued

--- page 22 ---
6) Shareholder Returns Policy
Our basic policy is to return capital directly to shareholders through dividends based on our highly-recurring cash 
generation capability, while also executing share repurchases flexibly. Over the three years of MTMP2026, we 
steadily enhanced base profit and strengthened the foundation supporting sustainable growth. Reflecting this 
expansion, under MTMP 2029 we will continue a progressive dividend policy, maintaining or increasing dividends, 
with a minimum full-year dividend of 140 yen per share, representing our largest-ever increase of 25 yen per share.*
In addition, we assume that cumulative shareholder returns as a percentage of COCF, including dividends and share 
repurchases, will be at the 50% level for the three-year period of MTMP2029.
* This is based on the assumption that the year-end dividend will be resolved at the Ordinary General Meeting of 
Shareholders on June 17, 2026
(4) Profit Distribution Policy
For further information regarding the shareholder returns policy, see “4. Corporate Information, 3. Dividend Policy.”

The table below shows assumptions for key commodity prices and foreign exchange rates for the forecast for the year 
ending March 31, 2027. The effects of movements in each commodity price and foreign exchange rates on profit for 
the year attributable to owners of the parent are included in the table.
*1   Set based on a Brent price assumption of USD 78/bbl.
*2   As the crude oil price affects our consolidated results with a time lag, the effect of crude oil prices on consolidated 
results is estimated as the consolidated oil price, which reflects this lag. For FY March 2027, we have assumed 
that there is a 4-6 month time lag for approx. 55%, a 1-3 month time lag for approx. 40%, and no time lag for 
approx. 5%. The above sensitivities show the annual impact of changes in the consolidated oil price.
*3   Actual sensitivity (includes the effects of hedging).

annual impact of changes in the weighted average sale price.

Fe 62%, the result was USD103/ton.

spot indicated price (Premium HCC FOB Australia) recorded in an industry trade magazine from April 2025 to 
March 2026.

companies denominated in their respective functional currencies and the impact of dividends received from major 
foreign investees. Depreciation of the yen has the effect of increasing profit through the conversion of profit 
(denominated in functional currencies) into yen. In the overseas affiliated companies where sales contracts are in 
USD, the impact of currency fluctuations between USD and the functional currency of AUD, and the impact of 
currency hedging, are not included.
Note: Impact of Foreign Currency Exchange Fluctuation on Operating Results
The total sums for profit attributable to owners of the parent for the years ended March 31, 2026 and 2025 reported by 
overseas subsidiaries and equity accounted investees were ¥781.1 billion and ¥754.0 billion, respectively. These 
companies principally use the US dollar and the Australian dollar as functional currencies in their reporting. We 
conducted a simplified estimation for the effect of foreign currency exchange fluctuations on profit for the year 
attributable to owners of the parent for the fiscal year ending March 31, 2027.
(a) More specifically, in the forecasting process we aggregated the total projected profit for the year attributable to 
owners of the parent in the business plans of these overseas affiliated companies for the fiscal year ending March 
31, 2027, based on each of their functional currencies. Firstly, we aggregated US dollar- and Australian dollar-
denominated projected profit attributable to owners of the parent. The impact of exchange rate fluctuations was

assessed based on the sum of the forecasted profit attributable to owners of the parent for overseas affiliated

profit attributable to owners of the parent. Likewise, a 1 yen change in the AUD/JPY exchange rate would have a 
net impact of ¥1.8 billion on profit attributable to owners of the parent.

Australian dollar as the functional currency. This impact should be considered separately from the sensitivity of the 
total profit for the year attributable to owners of the parent in each of the two currencies described in (a) to the yen 
equivalence valuation.

production companies, carry out hedging on the exchange rates between their functional currencies and the US 
dollar, which is the contractual currency for sales contracts. There are also cases that they carry out exchange rate 
hedging for yen equivalence valuation of profit for the year attributable to owners of the parent that is denominated 
in foreign currencies. It is necessary to take the impact of these factors into consideration separately from the 
sensitivity resulting from the yen equivalence valuation of profit for the year attributable to owners of the parent in 
each of the two currencies mentioned in (a) above.

2. Disclosure of Sustainability-related Financial Information
(1) Sustainability Policy
Mitsui & Co.’s corporate mission as defined in its Mission, Vision, Values (MVV) is to realize a better tomorrow for 
earth and for people around the world and to “Build brighter futures, everywhere.” Based on the MVV, we regard 
sustainability initiatives as important management issues, and, in accordance with the Mitsui & Co. Group Conduct 
Guidelines -With Integrity, this policy and sustainability-related policies, place a strong emphasis on sustainability in 
our corporate management. The Group will take on the challenge of addressing global issues and contributing to the 
realization of a sustainable society and economic growth through our business activities.
- Identification of Materiality and Promotion of Initiatives
For the sustainable growth of both society and the Company, we identify important business issues that impact the 
Company and its stakeholders as material issues (Materiality.) We put this policy into practice by formulating our 
business policies and strategies, including medium-term management plans and business plans, based on our 
Materiality, given that each material issue may become a significant source of both risks and opportunities in the 
medium to long term.
- Role of the Board of Directors
The Board of Directors appropriately supervises the Company’s sustainability initiatives, striving to enhance the 
Company’s corporate value in the medium to long term. Decisions on important issues related to sustainability are 
subject to final approval by or are reported to the Board of Directors, following a review by the Sustainability 
Committee and Executive Committee.
- Stakeholder Engagement and Information Disclosure
We regard dialogue with stakeholders as highly important. We strive to implement appropriate disclosure of 
information and respond sincerely and faithfully to the trust and expectations of our stakeholders.
(2) Materiality
In pursuit of our Mission, “Build brighter futures, everywhere,” we identify our materiality and are promoting corporate
activities. Since materiality consists of matters that may become risks or opportunities over the medium to long term,
we position it as a core axis in formulating our business policies and strategies, including our Medium-term
Management Plan and business plans.
In light of the perspective of double materiality*, and through dialogue with internal and external stakeholders as well
as discussions at the Board of Directors, we have identified the following six materialities. In addition, from May 2025,
we have positioned human rights as an independent materiality, recognizing it as an important issue concerning all
people involved in our corporate activities.
We have established Materiality Action Plans which set out specific policies, targets, initiatives and current status
regarding each materiality for each organization and we manage and disclose progress based on these plans. For
details on the Materiality Action Plans, see Sustainability Report 2025.
Sustainability Report 2025:
 https://www.mitsui.com/jp/en/sustainability/sustainabilityreport/2025/pdf/en_sustainability_2025.pdf
Materiality > Materiality Action Plans on our Sustainability website.
 https://www.mitsui.com/jp/en/sustainability/materiality/action_plans/materiality.html

Mitsui will also conduct business and activities by linking materiality and SDGs to continue to contribute to the United
Nations’ 17 Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs).
* The concept of considering importance from two perspectives: the financial impact the environment and society has 
on a company, and the impact of corporate activities on the environment and society.
(3) Sustainability Information
As described above, we face a wide range of sustainability issues. Among them, we have identified climate change 
response, supply chain and human rights, information security, and human resources strategy as major risks, 
considering the frequency of occurrence, expected scale of damage, and company-wide risk tolerance, as of the end 
of the current fiscal year under “3. Risk Factors.” Please refer to “(5) Climate Change Response,” “(6) Supply Chain 
and Human Rights,” “(7) Information Security,” and “(8) Human Resources Strategy” for the status of our response to 
these risks. Please refer to our Sustainability Report 2025 for information on natural capital and local communities.

in order to continuously strengthen our management foundation from a long-term perspective. Under “sustainability

such as climate change, natural capital, and business and human rights. For details of the Medium-term Management

(4) Governance Related to Climate Change Response and Supply Chain and Human Rights
The status of our climate change response and governance related to supply chain and human rights is described 
below.
- As stated in the Sustainability Policy disclosed on the Company’s website, the Board of Directors defines its role as 
overseeing initiatives related to climate change (including risks and opportunities) and human rights, and strives to 
enhance corporate value over the medium to long term. It clearly stipulates that basic management policies, 
business activities and corporate policies and strategies relating to sustainability are planned and proposed by the 
Sustainability Committee, a subordinate organization of the Executive Committee, and are decided upon after being 
submitted to or reported to the Board of Directors via the Executive Committee. In the fiscal year ended March 31, 
- 26 -
2. Operating and Financial Review and Prospects 2. Disclosure of Sustainability-related Financial Information (2) Materiality   continued

--- page 30 ---
2026, the Sustainability Committee (which met a total of seven times) deliberated and reported on climate change-
related targets and the progress, responses relating to mandatory disclosure requirements, and the enhancement of 
the human rights management framework and initiatives. The Sustainability Committee is chaired by the Chief 
Strategy Officer (CSO), a Representative Director, who is responsible for Corporate Staff Divisions such as 
Corporate Sustainability Div., and is composed of several Directors, chief operating officers of business units, and 
General Managers of the Corporate Staff Divisions appointed by the President and Chief Executive Officer. In 
addition, full-time Audit & Supervisory Board Members participate as observers.
- The Chair of the Sustainability Committee determines whether matters handled by the Sustainability Committee 
should be referred or submitted to the Executive Committee. In addition, as a Director, the CSO reports 
sustainability-related agenda items to the Board of Directors and, as a member of the Portfolio Management 
Committee, which is one of the advisory committees to the Executive Committee, also formulates portfolio strategies 
and draft investment and loan policies. The Sustainability Committee and the Portfolio Management Committee work 
in close coordination to ensure that portfolio management incorporates sustainability considerations. The CSO 
receives regular reports approximately once a month from the Corporate Sustainability Division, which is responsible 
for collecting and analyzing sustainability-related information and for planning and implementing various initiatives.
- In accordance with internal regulations, the Board of Directors resolves on basic management policies, matters 
concerning significant business execution, matters authorized by shareholders’ resolutions, and other matters 
stipulated by laws and regulations and the Articles of Incorporation, and receives reports on the status of significant 
business execution. Among these, handling policies regarding climate change (including risks and opportunities) and 
compliance with basic policies on human rights are subject to reporting as material sustainability matters. These 
principal agenda and reporting items are disclosed in integrated reports. In the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, in 
addition to the regular biannual reports on sustainability promotion activities at Board of Directors meetings, the 
“Outlook on GHG initiatives (progress toward GHG reduction targets and status of Global Energy Transition 
initiatives)” was reported to Directors and Audit & Supervisory Board Members, including External Members.
- We select candidates for Directors based on the appointment criteria set forth in the “Corporate Governance and 
Internal Control Principles.” In the selection process, after the Nomination Committee confirms that candidates 
satisfy the appointment criteria, the matter is submitted to the Board of Directors for deliberation. The appointment 
criteria broadly prescribe the capabilities, qualities, and other attributes required of our Directors as management 
executives. As our Sustainability Policy stipulates that the role of the Board of Directors is to appropriately oversee 
the Company’s sustainability initiatives, we believe that the capabilities, qualities, and other attributes set forth in the 
appointment criteria also apply to sustainability initiatives. Accordingly, in the process of selecting candidates for 
Directors, while comprehensively considering each candidate’s professional background, experience, capabilities, 
and qualities, we also consider, as one of the criteria, whether the candidate possesses the appropriate skills and 
competencies to oversee strategies designed to address risks and opportunities related to climate change and 
human rights.
- We have established a Sustainability Advisory Board composed of external experts and utilize the information and 
advice provided by its members in deliberations by the Sustainability Committee. During the fiscal year ended March 
31, 2026, including meetings held online, we held a total of five meetings to consult and exchange views on 
stakeholder engagement, including the status of dialogue initiatives and related issues and future vision; a total of 
two meetings on reviewing disclosures and on integrated initiatives relating to “climate change,” “natural capital,” and 
“human rights”; and one meeting each on the sustainability report, the 30th Conference of the Parties to the United 
Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (COP30), and the status of initiatives relating to natural capital, 
related issues, and future direction.
- To promote sustainability in our corporate management, we believe it is crucial to conduct dialogue with various 
stakeholders and respect their opinions regarding our business activities, and we conduct stakeholder dialogue 
every year. In the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, we invited Professor Yuya Kajikawa of the Institute for Future 
Initiatives at the University of Tokyo, and the members of the Sustainability Committee actively exchanged opinions 
with him on the role the Company should play, including its role and contributions in international and collaborative 
rule-making as an orchestrator of the business ecosystem, toward resolving various social issues including climate 
change and natural capital.

(5) Climate Change Response
The materiality identified by the Company includes “Establish a foundation for sustainable and stable supply,” “Create 
a community coexisting with nature,” and “Foster a well-being society.” Our Environmental Policy stipulates that we 
will pursue the kinds of business that will help society act to reduce greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions, as well as to 
mitigate and adapt to climate change. Following the Medium-term Management Plan 2026, we have positioned 
climate change as one of the key themes of our sustainability management in our Medium-term Management Plan 
2029. Through our wide-ranging business activities, we will contribute to the development of economies and societies 
in many countries across the world and to solutions to the global challenges we face, such as mitigating and adapting 
to climate change, thereby contributing to the goals of the Paris Agreement and Japan’s own long-term GHG emission 
reduction targets.
Specifically, 1) Governance, 2) Strategy, 3) Risk Management, and 4) Metrics and Targets related to our response to 
climate change are as follows.
1) Governance
In addition to “(4) Governance Related to Climate Change Response and Supply Chain and Human Rights,” we have 
implemented the following additional governance initiatives regarding climate change response.
- Since endorsing the Task Force on Climate-related Financial Disclosures (TCFD) in December 2018, we have 
conducted scenario analysis. From the fiscal year ended March 31, 2023, we have incorporated this analysis into 
our business planning process, and following its reporting and deliberation at the Executive Committee, reflected it in 
business plans approved by the Board of Directors. Through this process, the Board of Directors takes climate-
related risks and opportunities into account in its decision-making. In addition, in the consideration and review of 
business investments, we evaluate climate-related impacts from both risk and opportunity perspectives, and analyze 
the degree of such impacts through scenario analysis particularly for businesses with high GHG emissions or 
businesses significantly affected by climate change responses. In integrated risk management, transition risks and 
physical risks have been identified as material risks. After being discussed by the Portfolio Management Committee, 
they are reported and deliberated by the Executive Committee, and subsequently reported to the Board of Directors.
- The Board of Directors establishes the policy for determining individual Directors’ remuneration, etc. following 
deliberation and recommendations by the Remuneration Committee. In addition to basic remuneration and results-
linked bonuses, ROE and ESG factors, including climate change response, are used as evaluation items for 
performance-linked restricted stock-based remuneration, which constitutes medium- to long-term incentive 
remuneration. As environmental (“E”) factors, we use halving GHG Impact by 2030, halving GHG emissions (non-
consolidated and consolidated subsidiaries Scope 1 and 2 (excluding unincorporated joint ventures)) by 2030, and 
reducing gross GHG emissions (Scope 1 and 2 (including unincorporated joint ventures) and Scope 3 Category 15 
(investments)) by 30% by 2030 as indicators, and the amount paid varies from 80% to 120% according to the 
degree of achievement. In addition, the appropriateness and progress of management indicators and targets are 
regularly reviewed by the Remuneration Committee and the Board of Directors, and are revised as necessary in 
consideration of the global environment surrounding the Company.
2) Strategy
- We are conducting scenario analysis in short-, medium-, and long-term timeframes up to 2050. In identifying 
transition risks and opportunities, we refer to scenarios set out in the World Energy Outlook (WEO) published by the 
International Energy Agency (IEA), etc., and also referring to RCP (Representative Concentration Pathways) 
adopted by the IPCC (Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change) for the identification of physical risks.
- Transition risk analysis is regularly conducted in the business planning process, including the formulation of 
consolidated financial forecasts, and the results of the analysis are reflected in our business portfolio strategy. In 
consideration of scale of business operations and climate change impact (GHG emission amount or reduction 
contribution amount), we have selected the following 10 areas as high priority areas of business for scenario 
analysis: oil & gas and LNG, metallurgical coal, thermal power generation, iron ore, offshore oil & gas production 
facilities, gas distribution, LNG shipping, renewable energy, next-generation energy, and forest resources.
- For the upstream oil & gas and LNG, metallurgical coal, and thermal power generation, which we have judged to be 
particularly important in terms of scale of business operations and climate change impact, we have analyzed the 
impact on profit for the fiscal years ending March 31, 2030, 2040, and 2050 and presented them in three levels 
based on our assumed base case, taking into account our understanding of the business environment and various 
scenarios.

- On the other hand, with regard to physical risk, in order to verify the adequacy of the current risk response, we

the physical risk of flooding (flooding from inland waters, flooding from rivers, storm surge flooding), severe cold, 
extreme heat, tropical cyclones, landslides, wildfires, and water stress (water shortage) and drought under the 4°C 
scenario in 2030 and 2050.
- Together with our partners and customers, we are advancing initiatives in every industry in our broad range of 
businesses, from upstream to downstream in the value chain, to cut emissions throughout society. Furthermore, we 
have calculated Scope 3 emissions for the purpose of understanding the GHG emissions of the entire value chain.
- Please refer to “Disclosure Based on TCFD Recommendations - Transition Risk Assessments” on our Sustainability 
website below for the results of our transition risk analysis and physical risk analysis, as well as GHG reduction 
efforts along the value chain.
 https://www.mitsui.com/jp/en/sustainability/environment/climate_change/pdf/tcfd_en_202512.pdf
3) Risk Management
- The Company identifies risks on a company-wide basis and implements initiatives to identify material risks and avoid 
or control them. As part of this structure, we have established an integrated risk management framework to centrally 
manage risks on a company-wide basis, centered on the Executive Committee and the Portfolio Management 
Committee, which is one of its advisory committees. Under the integrated risk management framework, the 
department in charge of corporate staff divisions, which serves as the administrative office, is responsible for 
company-wide risk oversight. The material risks we anticipate include climate change risks, compliance risks, and 
risks related to infectious diseases, natural disasters, and terrorism, among others, including those related to 
environmental, social, and governance matters. In particular, we position risks arising from climate change (physical 
and transition) to be on the same level of importance as investment risks, geopolitical risks, and country risks, and 
implement measures to address them.
- We utilize both qualitative and quantitative methods, including monitoring GHG emissions, scenario analysis, risk 
interviews, dialogue with external experts, and reviews of industry trends. Given that we operate a wide range of 
businesses across countries and regions worldwide, we regard the diverse risks and opportunities associated with 
climate change as one of the important factors that must be taken into account in formulating our business 
strategies. We identify risks and opportunities associated with climate change across the short, medium, and long 
terms, conduct regular reviews, revise them in response to changes in the internal and external business 
environments, such as changes in environmental conditions and trends in each segment and portfolio replacement, 
and reflect them in our business strategies.

- In April 2020, we introduced an internal carbon pricing system to improve the medium- to long-term resilience of 
businesses that emit large amounts of GHGs and to promote businesses that contribute to GHG emission reductions 
at the Company and in society.
Disclosure on Measurement Methods for GHG Emissions, etc.
The Group measures Scope 1 emissions and Scope 2 emissions with reference to the “The GHG Protocol 
Corporate Accounting and Reporting Standard (2004)” (hereinafter, the “GHG Protocol (2004)”).
-Approach to Determining Organizational Boundaries for GHG Emissions-
  In measuring greenhouse gas emissions, the Group uses the operational control approach in determining 
organizational boundaries in order to clarify the relationship with the Company's proactive business activities. 
Under this approach, GHG emissions from consolidated subsidiaries and other entities over which we exercise 
operational control are classified under our Scope 1 and 2, while GHG emissions from investees over which we do 
not exercise operational control are classified under Scope 3 Category 15 (Investments).
  In connection with the application of the operational control approach, GHG emissions related to joint operations 
(Un-inco JVs) over which the Company does not have operational control with respect to GHG emissions, etc., 
which were previously classified under Scope 1 and 2, have been classified under Scope 3 Category 15 
(Investments) for the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026. As a result, GHG emissions, primarily in Scope 3 
Categories 10 and 11, have decreased. Similarly, GHG emissions related to time charter contracts, etc. over which 
we exercise operational control, which were previously classified under Scope 3 Categories 4 and 9, are classified 
under Scope 1 and 2.
  In addition, the Group’s Scope 3 Category 15 (Investments) was previously calculated using “the Mineral & Metal 
Resources, Energy, thermal power generation businesses, and all other equity method investee businesses” as the 
organizational boundary. However, for the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, the organizational boundary has been 
changed to “equity method investees, joint operations over which we do not have operational control (Un-inco JVs), 
and all significant business investees,” and GHG emissions are calculated accordingly.
-Methods for Measuring GHG Emissions, etc.-
  The Group measures GHG emissions, etc. using the following methods.
(a) Scope 1 emissions
  The sources of Scope 1 emissions within the Group are fuel combustion associated mainly with the operation of 
owned vessels and vehicles and with manufacturing and processing processes of consolidated subsidiaries.
  The Group measures Scope 1 emissions for the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026 based on an estimation method 
by multiplying the consumption of each fuel, gas, etc. by the emission factors set forth in the Calculation Tools and 
Guidance of the GHG Protocol (2004).
(b) Scope 2 emissions
  The primary source of Scope 2 emissions within the Group is electricity consumption.
  Beginning with the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, the Group discloses Scope 2 emissions based on the market-
based method in addition to those based on the location-based method.
- Location-based method
  The Group measures Scope 2 emissions on a location-based method using an estimation method by multiplying 
the electricity consumption at each site for the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026 by the country-specific emission 
factors of the International Energy Agency (IEA) available at March 31, 2026.
- Market-based method
  The Group measure Scope 2 emissions on a market-based method using an estimation method by, in principle,

(c) Scope 3 emissions
  The Group classifies Scope 3 emissions by Scope 3 categories as defined in the “GHG Protocol Corporate Value 
Chain (Scope 3) Standard (2011)” and measures them using an estimation method based on factors such as 
activity data and emission factors. Because the value chain related to Scope 3 emissions is broad and depends on 
various inputs, emissions are calculated by combining primary data based on direct measurements with estimates 
using secondary data, such as reasonable industry-average data provided by third-party data providers, from 
among the available data.

(d) Reduction Contribution amount
  Reduction Contribution refers to the quantification, from a lifecycle assessment perspective, of the GHG emissions 
reduced or avoided where the products and services provided by the Group through its business contribute to the 
reduction or avoidance of third-party GHG emissions (Scope 1 and 2), using emissions from existing products and 
services as the baseline for comparison. The factors generating Reduction Contribution amount in the Group are 
reductions or avoidance of GHG emissions associated mainly with the operation of renewable energy and the 
creation of emissions credits.
  In calculating this figure, we refer to guidelines issued by the World Business Council for Sustainable Development 
(WBCSD), The Institute of Life Cycle Assessment, Japan (ILCAJ), and others. For renewable energy, we multiply 
power generation results by country-specific emission factors from the International Energy Agency (IEA) available 
at the end of the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, as well as by specific emission factors under the laws and 
regulations of each country. For emissions credits, we measure the applicable quantity at the time of issuance. As 
there are currently no standardized calculation rules for Reduction Contribution amount, we measure it using our 
own methodology.
(e) Absorption amount
  Absorption amount refers to the quantified amount of CO
2 absorbed by the Group in its forestry resources 
business. In calculating this figure, we measure the annual CO2 absorption amount. As there are currently no 
standardized calculation rules for absorption amount, we measure it using our own methodology.
-Reporting Period for GHG Emissions, etc.-
  The Group measures GHG emissions using the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026 (from April 1, 2025 to March 31, 
2026) as the reporting period. The Group obtains information on GHG emissions from companies in its value chain. 
For some of this information, with reference to Paragraph 64 of Theme-based Sustainability Disclosure Standard 
No.2 “Climate-related Disclosures” the reporting period differs from the Group’s reporting period.

(6) Supply Chain and Human Rights
As Mitsui & Co. Group conducts business globally in many countries and regions, we recognize that we must make 
efforts to respect human rights not only within our own company but also across our supply chain. Therefore, we regard 
respect for human rights in line with international standards as the foundation of our sustainability management. In 2020, 
we formulated a Human Rights Policy, and to date we have clearly stated our commitment to respect human rights in 
both the Mitsui & Co. Group Conduct Guidelines and our Business Conduct Guidelines for Employees and Officers of 
Mitsui & Co., Ltd., striving to identify and reduce human rights-related risks including those involving our business 
partners.
In May 2025, in view of rising social expectations regarding human rights, we newly added “Cultivate societies that 
respect human rights” as a separate material issue in our Materiality. This move has further clarified our commitment to  
respect human rights in our corporate activities and our expectation that various stakeholders, including business 
partners, understand and practice respect for human rights.
Specifically, 1) Governance, 2) Strategy, 3) Risk Management, and 4) Metrics and Targets related to our response to 
supply chain and human rights are as follows.
1) Governance
- For details of our governance structure related to the supply chain and human rights, please refer to “(4) Governance 
Related to Climate Change Response and Supply Chain and Human Rights.” The Board of Directors, in accordance 
with our Sustainability Policy, oversees our efforts to respect human rights and engages in discussions and decision-
making aimed at enhancing the Company’s medium- to long-term corporate value. The Sustainability Committee, a 
subordinate body of the Executive Committee, deliberates and drafts basic policies and strategies on sustainability 
management. Under our management process, matters deliberated by the Sustainability Committee are, after 
Executive Committee review, either submitted to or reported to the Board of Directors. In the fiscal year ended March 
31, 2026, the Sustainability Committee discussed important matters such as the human rights management 
framework and the strengthening of related initiatives.
2) Strategy
- As Mitsui & Co. Group conducts diverse businesses and operates complex supply chains globally, we recognize that 
there are risks that may affect our business operations, including disruptions to supply chains and reputational 
damage, arising from human rights issues within our own operations and across our supply chains (such as forced 
labor and child labor).
- To address these human rights risks, we are committed to respecting human rights across our business activities 
and business relationships in accordance with our internal policies, including the “Sustainability Policy.” In 
conducting our business, we take care not only to avoid infringing on human rights ourselves, but also to ensure that 
we do not contribute to human rights infringements by others through our supply chains and other business 
relationships. We respect human rights as set out in the International Bill of Human Rights, including the Universal 
Declaration of Human Rights, and in the core labor standards of the International Labour Organization (ILO) 
Declaration on Fundamental Principles and Rights at Work. We also support the United Nations Guiding Principles 
on Business and Human Rights and the Ten Principles of the United Nations Global Compact. Based on these 
international standards, we have established our Human Rights Policy, Environmental Policy, and Sustainable 
Supply Chain Policy.
- Furthermore, under Medium-Term Management Plan 2029, we position sustainability management as one of the key 
management foundations to be continuously strengthened, and identify Business and Human Rights as a key theme 
for further deepening sustainability management. Under this strategic policy, we will further strengthen our human 
rights initiatives across the entire Group, including the continuous identification, assessment, and response to risks 
within our own operations and supply chains, enhanced collaboration with internal and external stakeholders and 
greater awareness-raising among officers and employees, and the development of grievance mechanisms. Through 
these efforts, we aim to prevent and mitigate adverse human rights impacts. Going forward, we will further advance 
our initiatives to respect human rights and strive to enhance our long-term corporate value and sustainability.
3) Risk Management
- In the fiscal year ended March 31, 2020, we engaged an external expert to evaluate human rights risks in our supply 
chain, covering the products handled by the Company and its overseas trading affiliates, as well as the major 
business operations of our consolidated subsidiaries, in light of the international standards mentioned above. The 
evaluation concluded that there is generally a risk of human rights issues such as forced labor or child labor arising 
in transactions where commodities like food, clothing, and construction materials originate mainly from emerging 
countries in Southeast Asia, Africa, and South America. We identified these as “high-risk fields” and initiated human 
rights due diligence.
- Specifically, under the Group’s human rights due diligence process, we implement measures centered on the four 
processes of Awareness, Identification, Surveys, and Disclosure and Improvements, with the aim of understanding 
and resolving human rights issues in the supply chain, as shown in the chart below.
- 35 -
2. Operating and Financial Review and Prospects 2. Disclosure of Sustainability-related Financial Information (5) Climate Change Response   continued

--- page 39 ---
(a) Initiatives until the fiscal year ended March 31, 2025
- By the fiscal year ended March 31, 2023, the Company sent questionnaire surveys to major suppliers in the high-risk 
areas mentioned above and evaluated the responses. Although no serious human rights issues were identified, 
some suppliers had yet to establish a human rights policy, and others had an insufficient understanding of related 
laws and regulations. In response, we strove to mitigate human rights-related risks by further explaining the 
Company’s initiatives and expectations, and proactively collaborating with suppliers to ensure an understanding and 
the practice of respecting human rights throughout the supply chain.
- In the fiscal year ended March 31, 2024, in addition to the above initiatives, the Company launched new measures to 
mitigate and prevent human rights-related risks, including risks within the supply chain, and strengthen risk 
management. Specifically, our business units introduced a human rights-related checklist for affiliated companies to 
use during self-audits and internal auditing. Additionally, the Company added a human rights clause to the standard 
terms and conditions of its purchase contracts for products and commodities. Furthermore, in training modules used 
for various levels of employees (new hires and line managers), we added content on “Business and Human Rights” 
to our program to increase knowledge on respect for human rights, as a measure to deepen awareness.
- In the fiscal year ended March 31, 2025, the Company took additional steps to strengthen initiatives related to 
human rights in the supply chain. First, we revised the definition of high-risk areas and expanded the scope of 
human rights due diligence. Specifically, the high-risk areas were categorized based on “industrial risks,” “country of 
origin risks,” and “level of importance for the Company.” As a result of this review, in addition to the previous 
business areas of food, clothing, and construction materials, we newly added transactions involving products 
sourced from emerging countries in Southeast Asia, Africa, South America, and other regions, in areas such as 
mining, oil and gas, chemicals, and industrial metals, as subjects of human rights due diligence. Furthermore, to 
improve the effectiveness of human rights due diligence, we established the Rules on Human Rights Management 
and set out specific procedures for each stage of the due diligence process (Awareness, Identification, Investigation, 
and Disclosure and Improvements). We also created risk management structures at each business site along with 
support and monitoring structures for use by Corporate Staff Divisions (Corporate Sustainability Division).
(b) Initiatives in the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026
- In the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, we placed particular emphasis on the continuous implementation of human 
rights due diligence and the strengthening of monitoring. Questionnaire surveys were conducted for suppliers in 
high-risk areas identified in the fiscal year ended March 31, 2025, and a cumulative total of 630 responses were 
collected and analyzed, including those from the fiscal year ended March 31, 2025. As a result, no serious human 
rights issues were identified in the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026. Going forward, we will continue to enhance our 
human rights due diligence processes, led by each business unit, through measures such as optimizing 
questionnaire surveys and periodically reviewing the scope of coverage, thereby working to reduce human rights-
related risks across the entire supply chain.
- We also strengthened on-site surveys within our supply chain during the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026. Based 
on information obtained from the on-site survey of the palm oil supply chain in Malaysia conducted in the fiscal year 
ended March 31, 2025, which suggested the possibility of human rights issues, we carried out additional surveys 
targeting the same palm oil refinery as well as upstream plantations and mills. These surveys focused on the 
working conditions of migrant workers and the living conditions of their families. In conducting the surveys, we 
collaborated with the Roundtable on Sustainable Palm Oil (RSPO), the administrators of Malaysia’s national 
certification scheme (MSPO), as well as local social enterprises, and conducted on-site inspections and interviews 
with relevant stakeholders. As a result, no instances of human rights violations, including forced labor or child labor, 
were identified. At the same time, we recognize that region- and industry-specific issues persist, and as a company 
handling palm oil, we will continue to engage in dialogue and collaboration with local stakeholders to reduce human 
rights risks and build a sustainable supply chain.
- In addition, we have continued to promote sustainability-related risk management across the entire Group. For 
example, in the fishery business, we utilized our environmental and social risk heat maps and sustainability due 
diligence checklists to assess the status of environmental and social risks, including biodiversity, water resources, 
and labor conditions, at our affiliated companies. As a result, we confirmed that these companies continue to acquire 
and renew internationally recognized certifications such as Aquaculture Stewardship Council (ASC) and Best 
Aquaculture Practices (BAP). We also recognized that these businesses have a high dependence on natural capital, 
- 36 -
2. Operating and Financial Review and Prospects 2. Disclosure of Sustainability-related Financial Information (6) Supply Chain and Human Rights   continued

--- page 40 ---
and that land use change, water stress, and waste management are key risk areas. We will continue to work closely 
with Group companies and business partners to enhance the visualization and rigorous management of risks, and 
will further improve monitoring and management levels across the Group and the entire supply chain.
- From the perspective of strengthening risk management at the operational level, we have continuously conducted 
human rights training for officers and employees, as well as human rights seminars for business partners and Group 
officers and Group employees with the participation of external experts. Approximately 270 participants attended 
these programs in the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026. In addition, we have expanded the use of e-Learning 
modules (available in Japanese and English) for officers and employees of the Company and its overseas affiliates, 
including content on Business and Human Rights, and made certain modules mandatory from the fiscal year ended 
March 31, 2026, to ensure participation. These awareness-raising initiatives have contributed to enhancing 
employees’ understanding and awareness of respect for human rights, and to the prevention and mitigation of 
human rights-related risks in our business.
- We have also focused on enhancing our grievance mechanism for addressing inquiries and complaints related to 
human rights. In addition to the grievance channel available on our website for receiving complaints and reports 
regarding human rights concerns involving the Company and its business partners, we began accepting complaints 
through a platform provided by a third-party organization, the Japan Center for Engagement and Remedy on 
Business and Human Rights (JaCER), in April 2026. Complaints and reports received through these internal and 
external channels are handled with due consideration for the anonymity of the reporter and the confidentiality of 
information. The Corporate Sustainability Division serves as the point of contact and coordinates with relevant 
business units and corporate staff divisions to address each case responsibly. Where necessary, we investigate the 
facts and request corrective actions from relevant parties as part of our efforts to improve and remediate issues. 
Through these initiatives, we aim to enable the early identification and response to human rights-related risks from 
both internal and external sources, thereby preventing the occurrence of human rights violations and ensuring 
appropriate remediation. We also regularly review the effectiveness of our grievance mechanism based on feedback 
from internal and external stakeholders and make improvements to its operation as necessary.
4) Metrics and Targets
- In the Medium-term Management Plan 2026, the Company set forth a basic policy of strengthening initiatives 
regarding the respect for human rights in business activities. On this basis, we identified the expansion of the scope 
of human rights due diligence, proactive collaboration with suppliers, and the enhancement of internal processes as 
our primary goals, executing measures to achieve specific KPIs. In Medium-term Management Plan 2029, we will 
maintain and further promote these goals, working to expand the scope of human rights due diligence, continuously 
operate the risk management system for suppliers and Group companies, and further enhance the system for 
stakeholder dialogue and monitoring.
- The Company continues to set a quantitative target of ensuring that 100% of new suppliers to the Company and its 
consolidated subsidiaries are made aware of the Sustainable Supply Chain Policy. When procuring goods and 
services, we send the policy to all new business partners and communicate our expectations in terms of human 
rights, the environment, labor and other issues.
- For the following four types of raw materials and commodities in fields that pose a high risk of deforestation, 
environmental impact, or human rights violations, in particular, we collaborate proactively with NGOs and other 
stakeholders to formulate a procurement policy for each specific commodity, while disclosing our targets and results 
for traceability and certified product procurement rates for each one. In the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, the 
Company formulated new procurement policies for coffee beans and cocoa beans. The Company will continue to 
expand the scope of target products and fields as necessary.

(7) Information Security
The Mitsui & Co. Group has established the following Information Security Policy, and performs risk management 
related to information security.
- Information Security Policy
(a) Approach toward Information Security
Mitsui recognizes the importance of information security, and by establishing and implementing relevant 
regulations, it shall conduct appropriate management of information assets, including information and IT 
systems, for the purpose of timely and effective use of information in compliance with “Mitsui & Co., Ltd. 
Corporate Governance and Internal Control Principles,” while striving to continuously improve such 
management on a global and group-wide basis.
(b) Compliance with Regulations (Establishing Compliance)
Mitsui shall comply with regulations, established standards, and other codes related to information security, and 
shall work towards the establishment and maintenance of compliant and suitable information security.
(c) Protection of Information Assets
Mitsui shall implement appropriate management for ensuring the confidentiality, integrity and availability of 
information assets, and work to protect its information assets from all possible threats.
(d) Response to Accidents
While working to prevent the occurrence of any accidents related to information security, in the unlikely event of 
an accident, Mitsui shall promptly take appropriate response measures including preventative steps against the 
reoccurrence of such an accident.
The specific 1) governance, 2) strategy, 3) risk management and 4) metrics and targets related to information security 
are as follows.
1) Governance
- 38 -
2. Operating and Financial Review and Prospects 2. Disclosure of Sustainability-related Financial Information (6) Supply Chain and Human Rights   continued

--- page 42 ---
The important principles for our global group information strategy are formulated in line with the management policy 
through the discussions at the Information Strategy Committee, as an advisory body under the control of the 
Executive Committee, which was established pursuant to the “Rules on the Information Strategy Committee,” and is 
chaired by the Chief Digital Information Officer (CDIO).
The Information Strategy Committee met nine times in total in the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026. The Committee 
discussed the revision of various rules based on the "Digital Grand Design," which materializes the ideal state of our 
global group systems, as well as cybersecurity initiatives based on the Mitsui Cybersecurity Strategy to respond to 
cyberattacks, initiatives to promote Data Driven Management, next-generation HR and general affairs systems, IT/DX 
R&D, and the Strategic DX Support Program, among other topics.
Under the system centered around the Information Strategy Committee, we are enhancing internal control including 
management of various possible risks such as information leakage and cyberattacks through maintenance of the 
following rules, necessary in light of development and operation of information systems and information security.
- “Rules on Information System Management”: Rules on the process of procurement, introduction and operation of 
information assets.
- “Rules on IT Security”: Code of conduct for the system supervisory divisions regarding IT security.
- “Rules on Information Management”: Basic policies in terms of information risk management system and information 
management.
- “Rules on Personal Information Protection”: Rules for the handling of personal information required for business 
execution (applied only in Japan).
- “Rules on Cybersecurity Countermeasures”: Rules for preventive measures against cyberattacks and emergency 
countermeasures in the event of incident.
- “Mitsui & Co. Group Cybersecurity Standards”: Basic cybersecurity measures aimed at being implemented in 
common across Mitsui & Co. Group companies.
Furthermore, the importance of cybersecurity measures in the Group is increasing as cyberattacks occurring day to 
day become more sophisticated, advanced and severe, such as targeted attacks on specific companies and 
organizations, ransomware (encrypting files and demanding ransom in exchange for their decryption), Business Email 
Compromise(BEC), and indiscriminate email attacks aimed at unspecified targets, and once a year, a report is made 
to the Board of Directors after deliberation in the Information Strategy Committee and the Executive Committee.
2) Strategy
Recognizing that cybersecurity risks in internal and external environments pose an increasingly serious threat, we 
formulated the Mitsui Cybersecurity Strategy in the fiscal year ended March 31, 2025, and are strengthening 
cybersecurity measures on a global group basis, as part of our overarching Digital Grand Design strategy, while 
drawing upon the knowledge of Mitsui Bussan Secure Directions, which is a dedicated cybersecurity subsidiary, and 
MITSUI KNOWLEDGE INDUSTRY, which is an information systems subsidiary.
- 39 -
2. Operating and Financial Review and Prospects 2. Disclosure of Sustainability-related Financial Information (7) Information Security   continued

--- page 43 ---
(a) <Top-down> Inclusion of cybersecurity in the management agenda
We position cybersecurity measures as one of the key elements of internal controls related to information security. In 
executing these measures, the Company involves senior management (particularly the management of group 
companies) and promotes them as part of group-wide integrated risk management. Specifically, reports are made and 
discussions are held in the Information Strategy Committee, the Executive Committee, and the Board of Directors 
every year, and awareness-raising activities are conducted for the management of affiliated companies through 
annual briefings and workshops.
(b) <Defensive> Ensuring company-wide minimum security measures (baseline)
We are promoting the establishment and optimization of a baseline for cybersecurity across the entire Group. 
Specifically, we draft and execute measures in accordance with the cybersecurity framework of the National Institute 
of Standards and Technology of the US, and take measures separated into the three steps of Hygiene, Protect, and 
Recovery while utilizing the knowledge of Mitsui Bussan Secure Directions.
Hygiene:   We believe in the importance of cyber hygiene, and engage in appropriate management of inventory 
for ascertaining the status of IT assets, and vulnerability management to identify and address potential 
entry points for attacks, as activities to maintain a healthy IT environment.
Protect:     Based on the approach of zero trust (security measures that perform verification without trusting any 
access to information assets and systems to be protected regardless of whether inside or outside the 
network), we are strengthening security measures in various IT areas such as IDs, devices, data, 
network and the cloud. Furthermore, we are building, maintaining and expanding a system for around-
the-clock global security monitoring and responding in the event of an incident.
Recovery: We have established the MBK-CSIRT (Computer Security Incident Response Team) as the center of 
security measures, working with cybersecurity leaders in each division to establish reporting and 
supporting systems, perform organized and continuous incident responses, and prevent recurrences. 
Furthermore, we have formulated response policies in the event of a security incident occurring 
according to the scale and severity of damage, and periodically conduct training to confirm 
effectiveness as needed.
(c) <Offensive> Maintaining and continuing business value and contributing to growth
We are actively enhancing our competitiveness and increasing added value by providing proactive, tailored support 
for cybersecurity measures related to our digital transformation (DX) initiatives. Specifically, we have established a 
dedicated consultation desk for security matters pertaining to DX projects, thereby offering advisory and support 
services from the project formation stage in accordance with the principles of “Security by Design.” In response to 
the rapid advancement of generative AI technologies, we are promoting the utilization of such technologies to 
improve productivity and enhance business value across the Company. We commenced the use of Microsoft 365 
Copilot as a company-wide AI platform in the fiscal year ended March 31, 2025, and have been expanding its 
utilization across the organization. Furthermore, we are advancing multiple generative AI projects tailored to 
specific operations and business domains. To ensure the secure use of generative AI, we have implemented a 
- 40 -
2. Operating and Financial Review and Prospects 2. Disclosure of Sustainability-related Financial Information (7) Information Security   continued

--- page 44 ---
range of risk mitigation measures. These include a comprehensive review of access rights to internal data, the 
formulation of risk guidelines for the use and provision of generative AI, and the mandatory completion of e-
learning programs outlining key precautions for AI usage. Additionally, when employees seek to use generative AI 
tools that fall outside the Company’s standard offerings, a formal application process is required. In such cases, we 
recommend the use of AI models that do not engage in retraining based on input data and that are not subject to 
monitoring of input/output data by service providers. Through these initiatives, we are contributing to the safe and 
effective utilization of digital transformation and generative AI technologies across Mitsui & Co. Group.
(d) <Bottom-up> Fostering a culture of established cybersecurity awareness
We are promoting the creation of a culture and atmosphere where security measures are considered “common 
sense” through education and awareness-raising for each level and role. Specifically, we have launched a 
“Cybersecurity Portal” for officers and employees including those of affiliated companies for the purpose of raising 
cybersecurity awareness and preventing the spread of damage from cyberattacks, and provide them with a variety 
of information such as the latest trends, case studies and appropriate actions and measures against cyberattacks 
to be taken by officers and employees. In addition, we prepare and utilize “Cybersecurity e-Learning” for end-users 
and security personnel respectively.
3) Risk Management
Risk related to information systems and information security is positioned as an important risk on “3. Risk Factors,” 
and the following steps are taken.
-We establish relevant rules for safety of information systems and strengthening information security, appropriately 
ensure the confidentiality, integrity and availability of information and information systems owned by Mitsui and its 
consolidated subsidiaries, and manage risks such as information leaks by continuously indicating guidelines for 
improving the level of risk management.
-We have established the “Mitsui & Co. Group Cybersecurity Standards” to be followed by group companies to 
strengthen cybersecurity measures in the Mitsui & Co. Group. Furthermore, a “Cybersecurity Baseline Survey” is 
conducted once per year in each affiliated company to self-assess the state of compliance with the standards and 
third-party evaluation is also performed through a “Cybersecurity Risk Assessment.”
-We have formulated response policies to security incidents in advance according to the scale and severity of damage 
as a cyber BCP (business continuity plan).
4) Metrics and Targets
We monitor the status of the implementation of group-wide fundamental cybersecurity measures as a key progress 
management indicator under the Mitsui & Co. Cybersecurity Strategy. We established the Mitsui & Co. Group 
Cybersecurity Standards in the fiscal year ended March 31, 2023 as basic cybersecurity measures aimed at being 
implemented in common across Mitsui & Co. Group. Mitsui designates significant affiliated companies for 
cybersecurity every year and monitors the state of compliance with the standards. All designated significant affiliates 
have completed self-assessments against the standards and finalized improvement plans, alternative measures, or 
risk acceptance policies for any outstanding items. As of the end of March 2026, the compliance rate stands at 100%.

The specific 1) Governance, 2) Strategy, 3) Risk Management and 4) Metrics and Targets related to human recourses

managing their compensation as human resource strategies aimed at maximizing human capital for executing 
business strategy, the CHRO also comprehends risks related to human capital such as employee turnover and
